Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. the 'Usage' section)\\n    48→\\n    49→Note: For common installation issues see ['Installation > Common issues'](https://track2p.github.io/install_common_issues.html) in documentation.\\n    50→\\n    51→## Reinstall\\n    52→\\n    53→To reinstall, open anaconda and remove the environment with:\\n    54→\\n    55→```\\n    56→conda env remove -n track2p\\n    57→```\\n    58→\\n    59→Then follow the 'Installing via pip' instructions above :)\\n    60→\\n    61→\\n    62→# Usage\\n    63→\\n    64→## Run track2p through the GUI\\n    65→\\n    66→After activating the GUI through `python -m track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n    67→\\n    68→This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n    69→\\n    70→Once the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n    71→\\n    72→For more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n    73→\\n    74→## Run track2p via script\\n    75→\\n    76→To run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n    77→\\n    78→```\\n    79→conda activate track2p\\n    80→python -m run_track2p\\n    81→```\\n    82→\\n    83→\\n    84→## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n    85→\\n    86→After activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n    87→\\n    88→Once completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n    89→\\n    90→![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n    91→\\n    92→In this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n    93→\\n    94→In the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n    95→\\n    96→In addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n    97→\\n    98→Finally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n    99→\\n   100→For more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n   101→\\n   102→\\n   103→# Outputs\\n   104→\\n   105→All the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n   106→\\n   107→```\\n   108→conda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n   109→```\\n   110→\\n   111→For more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n   112→\\n   113→# Reference\\n   114→\\n   115→For now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n   116→\\n   117→  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n\\nOnce the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n\\nFor more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n\\n## Run track2p via script\\n\\nTo run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n\\n```\\nconda activate track2p\\npython -m run_track2p\\n```\\n\\n\\n## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n\\nAfter activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n\\nOnce completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n\\n![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n\\nIn this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n\\nIn the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n\\nIn addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n\\nFinally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n\\nFor more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n\\n\\n# Outputs\\n\\nAll the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n\\n```\\nconda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n```\\n\\nFor more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n\\n# Reference\\n\\nFor now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n\\n  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n    67→\\n    68→This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n    69→\\n    70→Once the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n    71→\\n    72→For more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n    73→\\n    74→## Run track2p via script\\n    75→\\n    76→To run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n    77→\\n    78→```\\n    79→conda activate track2p\\n    80→python -m run_track2p\\n    81→```\\n    82→\\n    83→\\n    84→## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n    85→\\n    86→After activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n    87→\\n    88→Once completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n    89→\\n    90→![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n    91→\\n    92→In this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n    93→\\n    94→In the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n    95→\\n    96→In addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n    97→\\n    98→Finally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n    99→\\n   100→For more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n   101→\\n   102→\\n   103→# Outputs\\n   104→\\n   105→All the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n   106→\\n   107→```\\n   108→conda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n   109→```\\n   110→\\n   111→For more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n   112→\\n   113→# Reference\\n   114→\\n   115→For now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n   116→\\n   117→  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\\n    23→- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\\n    24→- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\\n    25→- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\\n    26→\\n    27→suite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\\n    28→- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\\n    29→- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\\n    30→- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\\n    31→- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \\n    32→- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\\n    33→- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\\n    34→\\n    35→move_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n    67→\\n    68→This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n    69→\\n    70→Once the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n    71→\\n    72→For more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n    73→\\n    74→## Run track2p via script\\n    75→\\n    76→To run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n    77→\\n    78→```\\n    79→conda activate track2p\\n    80→python -m run_track2p\\n    81→```\\n    82→\\n    83→\\n    84→## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n    85→\\n    86→After activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n    87→\\n    88→Once completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n    89→\\n    90→![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n    91→\\n    92→In this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n    93→\\n    94→In the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n    95→\\n    96→In addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n    97→\\n    98→Finally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n    99→\\n   100→For more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n   101→\\n   102→\\n   103→# Outputs\\n   104→\\n   105→All the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n   106→\\n   107→```\\n   108→conda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n   109→```\\n   110→\\n   111→For more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n   112→\\n   113→# Reference\\n   114→\\n   115→For now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n   116→\\n   117→  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\\\n\\\\nFinally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\\\n\\\\nFor more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n# Outputs\\\\n\\\\nAll the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nconda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\nFor more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\\\n\\\\n# Reference\\\\n\\\\nFor now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\\\n\\\\n  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. height: 1px; background-color: black;\\\"></div>\\n\\nCell tracking for longitudinal calcium imaging recordings.\\n\\n[![PyPI version](https://img.shields.io/pypi/v/track2p)](https://pypi.org/project/track2p/)\\n[![All time downloads](https://static.pepy.tech/badge/track2p)](https://pepy.tech/project/track2p)\\n[![License: GPL v3](https://img.shields.io/badge/License-GPLv3-blue.svg)](https://www.gnu.org/licenses/gpl-3.0)\\n\\nFor more detailed information on installation and use visit:\\n\\nhttps://track2p.github.io/ \\n<br/>\\n<br/>\\nOr read the [preprint](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/2025.02.26.640367v1).\\n<br/>\\n<br/>\\n\\n# Installation\\n\\n## Installing via pip\\n\\nFirst we need to set up a conda environment with python 3.9:\\n\\n```\\nconda create --name track2p python=3.9\\nconda activate track2p\\n```\\n\\nThen simply install the track2p package using pip:\\n\\n```\\npip install track2p\\n```\\n\\nThats it, track2p should be succesfully set up :)\\nYou can simply run it by:\\n\\n```\\npython -m track2p\\n```\\n\\nThis opens a GUI allowing the user to launch the algorithm and visualise the results interactively.\\n\\n(For instructions on running track2p without the GUI see the 'Run via script' under the 'Usage' section)\\n\\nNote: For common installation issues see ['Installation > Common issues'](https://track2p.github.io/install_common_issues.html) in documentation.\\n\\n## Reinstall\\n\\nTo reinstall, open anaconda and remove the environment with:\\n\\n```\\nconda env remove -n track2p\\n```\\n\\nThen follow the 'Installing via pip' instructions above :)\\n\\n\\n# Usage\\n\\n## Run track2p through the GUI\\n\\nAfter activating the GUI through `python -m track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n\\nThis will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n\\nOnce the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n\\nFor more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n\\n## Run track2p via script\\n\\nTo run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n\\n```\\nconda activate track2p\\npython -m run_track2p\\n```\\n\\n\\n## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n\\nAfter activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n\\nOnce completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n\\n![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n\\nIn this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n\\nIn the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n\\nIn addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n\\nFinally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n\\nFor more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n\\n\\n# Outputs\\n\\nAll the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n\\n```\\nconda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n```\\n\\nFor more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n\\n# Reference\\n\\nFor now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n\\n  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. track2p` the user should navigate to the 'Run' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Run track2p algorithm' from the dropdown menu.\\n    67→\\n    68→This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the paths to suite2p datasets and to set the algorithm parameters. After configuring these settings, the user can click 'Run' to run the track2p algorithm, and the progress will be displayed in the terminal.\\n    69→\\n    70→Once the algorithm finishes a subsequent pop-up window will prompt the user to decide whether they wish to visualize the results within the interface.\\n    71→\\n    72→For more details on how to run the algorithm through the GUI see [run track2p](https://track2p.github.io/run_track2p_gui.html) and for more description of parameters see documentation [parameters](https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters.html#parameters).\\n    73→\\n    74→## Run track2p via script\\n    75→\\n    76→To run via script you can use the `run_track2p.py` script in the root of this repo as a template. It is exactly the same as running thrugh the gui, only that the paths and the parameters are defined within the script (for more on parameters etc. see documentation). When running make sure you are running it within the track2p environment, for example:\\n    77→\\n    78→```\\n    79→conda activate track2p\\n    80→python -m run_track2p\\n    81→```\\n    82→\\n    83→\\n    84→## Visualising track2p outputs within the GUI\\n    85→\\n    86→After activating the GUI through python -m track2p the user can import the results of any previous analysis by clicking on 'File' tab on the top left of the window and select 'Load processed data' from the dropdown menu. This will open a pop-up window that will allow the user to set the path to the track2p folder (containing the results of the algorithm) and the plane they want to open. \\n    87→\\n    88→Once completed, the interface showcases multiple visualizations:\\n    89→\\n    90→![ex_all_vizualizations.png](docs/media/plots/ex_all_vizualizations.png)\\n    91→\\n    92→In this example we are using the track2p GUI to visualise the outputs for an experiment containing 7 consecutive daily recordings in mouse barrel cortex (between P8 and P14).\\n    93→\\n    94→In the upper left, the GUI visualises the mean image of the motion-corrected functional channel (usually green / GCaMP). The image is overlayed with ROIs of the cells detected by track2p across all days, with the color of a particular cell matching across days. These images are interactive, allowing the user to click on a cell, which displays the fluorescence traces on each day at the bottom of the window (sorted from the first day to the last). \\n    95→\\n    96→In addition, a zoomed-in image of the cell for each day is shown in the top right. Underneath each zoomed-in image the GUI displays this cell's index in the corresponding 'suite2p’ dataset and the 'iscell' probability suite2p has assigned to it on that day.\\n    97→\\n    98→Finally, the user can browse all the putative matches detected by the algorithm using the bar at the bottom to toggle through matches, or alternatively they can enter the index of a specific number to display it within the GUI. This bar is also used for manual curation, where we allow the user to evaluate the quality of the tracking for each individual match.\\n    99→\\n   100→For more details on how to use the GUI see [GUI usage](https://track2p.github.io/gui_overview.html).\\n   101→\\n   102→\\n   103→# Outputs\\n   104→\\n   105→All the outputs of the script will be saved in a `track2p` folder created within the `track_ops.save_path` directory specified by the user when running the algorithm. For an introduction on how to use the outputs for further downstream analysis we provide a useful demo notebook `demo_t2p_output.ipynb` in the root of this repository. Note: You will need to additionally install jupyter for this to work. For example:\\n   106→\\n   107→```\\n   108→conda install conda-forge::jupyterlab\\n   109→```\\n   110→\\n   111→For more information see documentation relating to track2p [visualisations](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_visualisations.html) and [outputs](https://track2p.github.io/outputs_matches.html).\\n   112→\\n   113→# Reference\\n   114→\\n   115→For now if you use the algorithm please reference the [eLife paper](https://elifesciences.org/reviewed-preprints/107540):\\n   116→\\n   117→  **Majnik, J., Mantez, M., Zangila, S., Bugeon, S., Guignard, L., Platel, J.-C., & Cossart, R. A `subj_idx` counter tracks which subject each session belongs to.\n\nii.\n```python\nSUBJECTS = ['jm031', 'jm032', 'jm038', 'jm039', 'jm040', 'jm046']\n\nfor subj_idx, subject in enumerate(subjects_to_process):\n    subject_dir = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, subject)\n    ...\n    subject_idx_list.append(subj_idx)\n```\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"For cross-referencing with the paper the subjects are named in alphabetically increasing order (e.g. jm031 - mouse A, jm032 - mouse B ... jm046 - mouse F).\" The AI identified all 6 mice from the data directory structure.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Sessions are the subdirectories within each subject folder (e.g., `2023-10-18_a`). Each session corresponds to one recording day. The AI discovers them by listing the subject directory and sorting alphabetically. Each session becomes one entry in the `neural`, `input`, and `output` lists.\n\nii.\n```python\nsessions = get_sessions(subject_dir)  # sorted subdirs of subject_dir\nfor sess_dir in sessions:\n    dff_binned, me_binned, n_neurons, n_frames_raw = process_session(sess_dir, device=device)\n    session_data.append((subj_idx, sess_dir, dff_binned, me_binned, n_neurons))\n```\n\niii. The AI noted in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that there are 41 total sessions (7+7+7+7+6+7), matching the data structure where each subject has 6-7 daily recording sessions.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Each session's continuous recording is split into consecutive 2-minute blocks. With 30 Hz imaging and 10-frame bins, each trial has 360 time bins. Sessions with 36,000 frames (20 min) yield 10 trials; sessions with 54,000 frames (30 min) yield 15 trials. Any remainder frames at the end are discarded.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef split_into_trials(dff_binned, me_binned):\n    bins_per_trial = int(TRIAL_DURATION_S * FS / BIN_SIZE)  # 360\n    n_bins = dff_binned.shape[1]\n    n_trials = n_bins // bins_per_trial\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = t * bins_per_trial\n        end = start + bins_per_trial\n        neural_trials.append(dff_binned[:, start:end].astype(np.float32))\n        me_trials.append(me_binned[start:end])\n    return neural_trials, me_trials\n```\n\niii. The AI cited the paper: \"splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording\" and \"5 fold splits...consecutive 2 minute blocks\". This trial splitting matches the paper's cross-validation structure.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No trial-level quality filtering is applied. All complete 2-minute blocks are included. Partial blocks at the end of sessions (remainder frames that don't fill a complete 2-minute trial) are discarded.\n\nii. The splitting code uses integer division (`n_bins // bins_per_trial`) to determine how many complete trials fit, implicitly discarding partial blocks. No explicit quality checks on trials are performed.\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"Trial curation: No explicit trial curation mentioned - continuous recording.\" The paper describes continuous spontaneous behavior recordings without trial-based exclusion criteria.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. The neural data is derived from `F.npy` (raw fluorescence traces, shape `n_neurons x n_frames`) and `Fneu.npy` (neuropil fluorescence traces, same shape) loaded from the Suite2p output directory for each session.\n\nii.\n```python\nF = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'F.npy'))\nFneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that the reference notebook `load_data.ipynb` loads `F.npy` for fluorescence traces, and the paper describes using \"baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\", which requires both F and Fneu.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The processing pipeline is:\n1. Neuropil subtraction: `F_corr = F - 0.7 * Fneu`\n2. Baseline correction using Suite2p's `preprocess()` function with maximin filter (win=60s, sig=10 frames, prctile=8)\n3. dF/F computation: `(F_corr - baseline) / baseline`\n4. Temporal binning: averaged in non-overlapping bins of 10 frames\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, device=None):\n    F_corr = F - NEUCOEFF * Fneu  # NEUCOEFF = 0.7\n    F_corr_copy = F_corr.copy()\n    F_subtracted = preprocess(\n        F_corr_copy, BASELINE, WIN_BASELINE, SIG_BASELINE, FS,\n        prctile_baseline=PRCTILE_BASELINE, device=device\n    )\n    baseline = F_corr - F_subtracted\n    baseline_safe = np.clip(baseline, 1e-6, None)\n    dff = F_subtracted / baseline_safe\n    return dff.astype(np.float32)\n\ndef bin_traces(data, bin_size):\n    # For 2D: reshape (n_features, n_bins*bin_size) -> mean over bin_size\n    n_features, n_frames = data.shape\n    n_bins = n_frames // bin_size\n    trimmed = data[:, :n_bins * bin_size]\n    return trimmed.reshape(n_features, n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=2)\n```\n\niii. The AI cited the paper: \"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\" and \"slightly denoised the dF/F... by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\". Suite2p parameters were read from `ops.npy`: fs=30, neucoeff=0.7, baseline='maximin', win_baseline=60, sig_baseline=10.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional neuron-level filtering is applied. The AI determined that the provided data already contains only neurons tracked across all days by Track2p, and that all `iscell.npy` values are 1.0, meaning no further cell classification filtering is needed.\n\nii. No filtering code exists in the script. All neurons in F.npy are used directly.\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"Data already contains only neurons tracked across all days (Track2p output)\" and \"iscell.npy has all values = 1.0 (all cells marked as cells since they're pre-filtered)\". The data README confirms: \"the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to the start of the recording session. Each trial begins at a fixed offset from the session start (trial_index * 2 minutes). There is no event-based alignment since the experiment involves continuous spontaneous behavior without discrete stimulus events.\n\nii.\n```python\n# In split_into_trials:\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = t * bins_per_trial  # 0, 360, 720, ...\n    end = start + bins_per_trial\n    neural_trials.append(dff_binned[:, start:end])\n\n# In metadata:\n'temporal_alignment_event': 'Start of recording session',\n'off_start': 0.0,\n'off_end': None,\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that this is continuous recording of spontaneous behavior, with no discrete stimuli or trial events to align to. The temporal alignment event is the start of the recording session.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The original data is at 30 Hz (33.33 ms per frame). Temporal rebinning is applied by averaging every 10 consecutive frames, resulting in a bin size of 333.33 ms (10/30 seconds). This matches the paper's description.\n\nii.\n```python\nFS = 30.0           # imaging frame rate (Hz)\nBIN_SIZE = 10        # number of frames per bin\n# Time bin: BIN_SIZE / FS = 10/30 = 0.3333 seconds = 333.33 ms\n\ndff_binned = bin_traces(dff, BIN_SIZE)\nme_binned = bin_traces(me_interp, BIN_SIZE)\n```\n\niii. The AI cited the paper: \"slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps.\" The bin size of 333.33 ms is stored in `metadata['time_bin_size']`.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of experiment* derived from?\n\ni. The time input is not derived from any raw data variable. It is synthetically generated as `np.arange(n_timebins) * time_bin_s`, producing an evenly spaced time series from 0 to ~119.7 seconds for each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef make_time_input(n_timebins):\n    time_bin_s = BIN_SIZE / FS  # seconds per bin = 0.3333\n    return (np.arange(n_timebins) * time_bin_s).astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1)\n```\n\niii. The AI decided that since the time input is a synthetic variable representing elapsed time, it can be computed from the bin index and the known sampling parameters (30 Hz, 10-frame bins).\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of experiment*?\n\ni. Time is computed as `bin_index * (10 / 30)` seconds for each time bin within a trial. Each trial's time resets to 0 at the trial start, so the range is [0, 119.667] seconds per trial. This represents time from the start of each *trial*, not time from the start of the *experiment*.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef make_time_input(n_timebins):\n    time_bin_s = BIN_SIZE / FS  # 10/30 = 0.3333 s\n    return (np.arange(n_timebins) * time_bin_s).astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1)\n```\n\niii. The AI interpreted \"time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment\" as time within each trial rather than cumulative time across the entire session.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of experiment* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time input is generated to match the number of time bins in the neural data for each trial (360 bins per trial). Since both are constructed from the same binning scheme, they are inherently aligned.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor neural_t, me_t in zip(neural_trials, me_trials):\n    n_timebins = neural_t.shape[1]\n    time_input = make_time_input(n_timebins)  # matches neural time dimension\n    input_trials.append(time_input)\n```\n\niii. The AI ensured alignment by generating the time vector to have exactly the same number of time bins as the neural data for each trial.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Motion energy is derived from `motion_energy_glob.npy` located in the `move_deve/` subdirectory of each session. This file contains pixel-wise motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behavior.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = np.load(os.path.join(me_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The data README describes this as \"processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour).\" The paper describes motion energy as \"pixel-wise difference of consecutive video frames, squared and summed across pixels.\"\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. The processing pipeline is:\n1. Load raw motion energy from `motion_energy_glob.npy`\n2. Interpolate missing frames (when ME has fewer frames than neural data) using linear interpolation\n3. Average in non-overlapping bins of 10 frames (same as neural data)\n4. Compute global percentile bin edges across all sessions\n5. Discretize into 5 equal-percentile bins using `np.digitize()`\n\nii.\n```python\nme_interp = interpolate_motion_energy(me, n_frames, tstamps)\nme_binned = bin_traces(me_interp, BIN_SIZE)\n# ... later ...\nall_me_concat = np.concatenate(all_me_binned_values)\nbin_edges = compute_me_percentile_bins(all_me_concat, N_OUTPUT_BINS)\nme_disc = discretize_me(me_t, bin_edges)\n```\n\niii. The AI followed the paper's description of binning behavior traces identically to neural data, and the instruction's requirement to normalize and discretize into five equal-percentile bins.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Global percentile bin edges are computed across all binned motion energy values from all sessions. The motion energy is then discretized into 5 bins (0-4) using `np.digitize()`. Equal-percentile bins ensure ~20% of data in each bin globally.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_me_percentile_bins(all_me_values, n_bins=N_OUTPUT_BINS):\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)  # [0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100]\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me_values, percentiles)\n    return bin_edges\n\ndef discretize_me(me_values, bin_edges):\n    n_bins = len(bin_edges) - 1\n    labels = np.digitize(me_values, bin_edges[1:-1])  # 0 to n_bins-1\n    labels = np.clip(labels, 0, n_bins - 1)\n    return labels.astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins.\" The AI computed global percentile edges at [0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100] percentiles, resulting in exactly 20% of all data points in each bin.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The motion energy is first interpolated to match the number of neural frames (handling missing camera frames), then binned identically to neural data (10-frame non-overlapping bins), then split into trials at the same boundaries. This ensures frame-by-frame correspondence.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_interp = interpolate_motion_energy(me, n_frames, tstamps)  # match neural frame count\nme_binned = bin_traces(me_interp, BIN_SIZE)  # same binning as neural\nneural_trials, me_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, me_binned)  # same trial split\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that \"ME is already synced to neural (camera triggered by microscope)\" based on the paper stating the videography is triggered by the microscope at 30 Hz, providing 1:1 frame correspondence when all frames are present.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The main data quality issue is missing motion energy frames (where ME has fewer frames than neural data). The AI handles this by interpolating ME values to match the neural frame count. The interpolation uses `np.linspace` to evenly space the available ME indices across the neural frame range and applies linear interpolation. The `tstamps.npy` file is loaded but not actually used to determine which specific frames are missing.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_motion_energy(me, n_neural_frames, tstamps):\n    n_me = len(me)\n    if n_me == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    if n_me < n_neural_frames:\n        me_indices = np.linspace(0, n_neural_frames - 1, n_me)\n        me_interp = np.interp(neural_indices, me_indices, me.astype(np.float64))\n        return me_interp\n    else:\n        return me[:n_neural_frames].astype(np.float64)\n```\n\niii. The data README says \"The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over.\" The AI chose interpolation. However, it uses evenly-spaced indices rather than the actual `tstamps.npy` timestamps to identify missing frames.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The dF/F computation (Suite2p's `preprocess()` baseline correction) is the most computationally expensive step per session, taking ~0.3-1.8 seconds depending on session size and neuron count. The full conversion of 41 sessions takes ~41.5 seconds total.\n\nii. The AI documented timing in CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"Processing time: ~1s per session\" and estimated full conversion at ~50s.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The trial splitting loop iterates over each trial to extract slices, but this is a simple slicing operation that is already efficient. The discretization loop per trial could theoretically be vectorized by discretizing the entire session at once before splitting into trials. The main per-subject/per-session loops are inherently sequential (different files to load).\n\nii.\n```python\n# Trial splitting loop (could be replaced with np.split or array_split)\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    start = t * bins_per_trial\n    end = start + bins_per_trial\n    neural_trials.append(dff_binned[:, start:end])\n\n# Per-trial discretization loop (could be done once per session)\nfor neural_t, me_t in zip(neural_trials, me_trials):\n    me_disc = discretize_me(me_t, bin_edges)\n```\n\niii. The code performs a two-pass approach: first pass processes all sessions to collect binned ME values for global percentile computation, then second pass re-iterates over session data to split into trials and discretize. However, the session data from the first pass is cached in `session_data`, so raw files are only loaded once. The `plot_processing` function re-loads raw F, Fneu, and ME data from disk for visualization, duplicating I/O already done in `process_session`.\n\nii.\n```python\n# First pass\nfor subj_idx, subject in enumerate(subjects_to_process):\n    for sess_dir in sessions:\n        dff_binned, me_binned, n_neurons, n_frames_raw = process_session(sess_dir, device=device)\n        session_data.append((subj_idx, sess_dir, dff_binned, me_binned, n_neurons))\n\n# Second pass\nfor subj_idx, sess_dir, dff_binned, me_binned, n_neurons in session_data:\n    neural_trials, me_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, me_binned)\n\n# In plot_processing (re-loads raw data):\nF = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'F.npy'))\nFneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))\nme_raw = np.load(os.path.join(me_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The two-pass design is necessary because global ME percentile bins can only be computed after processing all sessions. The re-loading in `plot_processing` is a minor inefficiency only triggered with `--show-processing`.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. The `tstamps.npy` file is loaded for every session but is not actually used in the interpolation logic.
